# **Chapter 3 --- The Invisible AI Problem** 

###  ******How AI quietly influences professional decisions**

In many professional environments today, artificial intelligence is not
experienced as a dramatic technological presence. It does not always
appear as an automated system that openly replaces human judgement or
takes control of a decision. Instead, its influence often emerges
quietly, embedded within everyday workflows in ways that may be subtle
and difficult to observe.

A researcher exploring a question may consult an AI system to summarise
a body of literature before deciding which papers to examine more
closely. A clinician may review machine-generated suggestions that
highlight possible interpretations of diagnostic information. A lawyer
may ask a system to outline potential arguments before constructing a
case. A policy analyst may use an intelligent system to generate
possible scenarios that frame further discussion.

In each of these situations, the machine does not make the final
decision. The professional remains responsible for determining the
appropriate course of action. Yet the machine's output influences the
reasoning process that leads to that decision.

This influence often occurs without any formal recognition that
artificial intelligence has become part of the decision environment.

Unlike traditional analytical tools, which typically perform clearly
defined operations such as statistical calculations or database queries,
modern AI systems generate outputs that resemble elements of human
reasoning. They summarise information, suggest interpretations, generate
explanations, and propose alternative perspectives. Because these
outputs are presented in a form that appears immediately understandable,
professionals may incorporate them into their thinking without pausing
to consider how they were produced or how they shape the reasoning
process.

The interaction becomes almost conversational.

A professional asks a question, receives a structured response, and then
decides how that response should inform the next step in their analysis.
The machine's contribution may be brief---a summary, a suggestion, or a
list of possibilities---but it influences what the professional notices,
what possibilities are considered, and how the problem is framed.

Over time, such interactions can become routine.

Once an intelligent system becomes available within a workflow,
professionals may naturally begin consulting it whenever they encounter
a complex question. The system becomes one of many resources used during
reasoning, alongside documents, data sources, and conversations with
colleagues. Because the interaction feels similar to other forms of
information gathering, its influence may not be treated as something
that requires explicit documentation or reflection.

This is how artificial intelligence can quietly become part of
decision-making.

The influence is rarely dramatic. It does not always involve the machine
producing a final answer or determining an outcome. Instead, it shapes
the path through which the professional arrives at a conclusion. A
suggestion generated by a system may lead a researcher to investigate a
particular variable. A summary may highlight certain arguments while
omitting others. A proposed explanation may guide attention toward one
interpretation of a problem rather than another.

These small shifts accumulate within the reasoning process.

The professional still evaluates the information, exercises judgement,
and ultimately decides how to act. Yet the reasoning process has been
influenced by machine-generated insight that was not present at the
beginning of the analysis.

Importantly, this influence often remains **invisible within the formal
record of the decision**.

When decisions are documented, the record typically includes the final
reasoning, the evidence considered, and the conclusions reached. Rarely
does it capture the informal interactions that occurred during the
exploration of the problem. If an AI system generated suggestions that
shaped the direction of analysis, those suggestions may not appear
anywhere in the documentation. The decision appears to be the product of
human reasoning alone, even though machine-generated insight contributed
to the path that led to it.

This invisibility does not necessarily arise from intentional
concealment. In many cases, professionals simply treat interactions with
intelligent systems as part of their exploratory thinking. Just as one
might consult a reference book or discuss an idea with a colleague,
consulting an AI system becomes another step in the process of
understanding a problem. Because these interactions occur early in the
reasoning process, before a formal conclusion has been reached, they may
not be seen as something that needs to be recorded.

Yet from the perspective of governance, these interactions matter.

When machine-generated insight influences how a problem is explored, it
becomes part of the system that produced the decision. Even if the final
judgement remains human, the reasoning process that led to that
judgement may have been shaped by suggestions that originated from a
machine.

Understanding this influence requires recognising that decision-making
rarely occurs as a single, clearly defined moment. Instead, it unfolds
through a sequence of explorations, interpretations, and revisions.
Professionals move back and forth between different sources of
information, gradually refining their understanding of the problem.
Artificial intelligence can enter this process at many points, subtly
shaping the questions that are asked and the interpretations that are
considered.

Because this influence occurs during the exploratory phase of reasoning,
it often escapes formal governance structures.

Policies and oversight mechanisms typically focus on systems that
produce final decisions or automated outcomes. They are designed to
ensure that specific technologies operate within defined boundaries. But
when AI participates informally within human reasoning, its influence
may not appear as the output of a system that can be easily monitored or
audited. Instead, it becomes woven into the cognitive process through
which professionals interpret information.

This quiet integration of machine-generated insight creates a new
challenge for institutions.

If artificial intelligence is influencing how professionals explore
problems, then governance must account for these interactions even when
they do not produce explicit outputs or automated decisions.
Institutions must consider not only how AI systems are deployed but also
how they shape the **process of reasoning itself**.

Recognising the presence of invisible AI influence is therefore a
crucial step in understanding the governance challenges introduced by
intelligent systems. Decisions may remain human in the sense that
professionals retain responsibility for their outcomes. But the
reasoning environments in which those decisions are formed are
increasingly shaped by interactions with machines capable of generating
insight.

The question that follows is not whether professionals should be allowed
to consult such systems. In many cases, these systems provide valuable
analytical support. The more important question is how institutions can
ensure that the influence of machine-generated insight remains
**visible, accountable, and compatible with responsible
decision-making**.

The next section examines why the invisibility of AI influence poses a
particular challenge for accountability within professional decision
systems.

### 

### **Why invisible AI influence undermines accountability**

Accountability is one of the most fundamental principles of professional
life. In medicine, law, research, public administration, engineering,
and many other fields, decisions must be explainable. When an important
action is taken---whether diagnosing a patient, approving a policy,
publishing a scientific conclusion, or allocating resources---there must
be a clear account of how that decision was reached and who is
responsible for it.

This expectation exists because professional decisions often have
consequences that extend far beyond the individual making them. They
affect patients, citizens, students, institutions, and societies.
Accountability therefore serves two essential purposes. It allows
decisions to be reviewed and evaluated, and it ensures that
responsibility for those decisions remains identifiable.

Traditionally, accountability has been supported by the fact that
professional reasoning occurs primarily within the minds of the people
making the decision. A clinician may consult medical literature, a
researcher may analyse data, or a policymaker may review reports and
expert opinions. Yet the reasoning that connects these sources of
information to the final decision ultimately belongs to the human
professional who interprets them. If questions later arise, that
reasoning can be explained, defended, or reconsidered.

Artificial intelligence complicates this structure in subtle but
important ways.

When machine-generated insights begin to influence professional
reasoning, the decision process no longer originates solely from human
cognition. Suggestions, summaries, or analytical interpretations
generated by intelligent systems may shape the way a problem is
understood or the direction in which an investigation proceeds. These
influences may be significant, yet they often leave no trace in the
record of the decision.

As a result, the reasoning process that produced the decision becomes
partially invisible.

Consider a researcher who consults an AI system while exploring a
complex dataset. The system proposes several possible patterns worth
investigating. The researcher examines these suggestions and decides to
pursue one of them further. Over time, this line of inquiry leads to a
published conclusion. In the final research report, the reasoning
appears coherent and well supported by evidence. The document explains
the statistical relationships observed in the data and the
interpretation derived from them.

What the report may not show is that the initial idea for examining a
particular variable emerged from a machine-generated suggestion. That
suggestion influenced the trajectory of the research process, yet it may
never appear in the formal documentation of the work.

A similar situation can arise in many other professional contexts. A
clinician may consider a diagnostic possibility highlighted by an
analytical system. A policy analyst may pursue a line of argument first
suggested by an AI-generated summary. A legal professional may structure
an argument based partly on machine-generated comparisons with earlier
cases.

In each case, the human professional remains responsible for evaluating
the suggestion and deciding how to act upon it. The final judgement is
still human. However, the path that led to that judgement has been
influenced by machine-generated insight that may not be recorded.

From the perspective of accountability, this creates a gap.

When decisions are later examined---whether through internal review,
legal scrutiny, or professional reflection---the record may show only
the final reasoning presented by the professional. The earlier
interactions with intelligent systems that shaped the direction of
thinking may be absent from the documentation. Observers attempting to
reconstruct the reasoning process may therefore see only part of the
system that produced the decision.

This gap does not necessarily imply misconduct or irresponsibility.
Professionals often use many sources of information while developing
their understanding of a problem. Conversations with colleagues,
informal reflections, and exploratory analysis all contribute to the
reasoning process without always being formally documented. Yet
artificial intelligence introduces a new category of influence that
differs from these traditional sources.

Unlike informal conversations or personal reflection, machine-generated
suggestions are produced by systems whose behaviour may depend on
complex models, training data, and algorithmic processes. These systems
can influence reasoning in ways that are difficult to reconstruct after
the fact. If the interaction between human and machine remains
undocumented, it may be impossible to determine how strongly the
machine's output shaped the final decision.

This ambiguity complicates the assignment of responsibility.

If a decision is later questioned, the professional involved may explain
the reasoning that appears in the final documentation. Yet that
explanation may omit earlier influences that guided the investigation
toward particular conclusions. The decision appears fully human in
origin, even though the reasoning environment included machine-generated
insight that influenced its development.

At the same time, it would be misleading to attribute responsibility to
the machine itself. Artificial intelligence systems do not possess
accountability in the way human professionals do. They generate outputs
based on patterns within data, but they do not exercise judgement,
understand consequences, or accept responsibility for outcomes.
Responsibility must therefore remain with the human actors who interpret
and act upon machine-generated information.

The difficulty lies in the space between these two realities. Human
professionals remain accountable, yet the reasoning process that
produced the decision may include influences that remain partially
hidden.

Invisible AI influence therefore weakens one of the key foundations of
accountable decision-making: the ability to reconstruct how a conclusion
was reached.

When reasoning processes cannot be fully reconstructed, it becomes
harder for institutions to evaluate whether decisions were made
responsibly. Peer reviewers may struggle to assess whether relevant
possibilities were considered. Investigators examining a contested
decision may find it difficult to determine how certain interpretations
gained prominence. Organisations attempting to learn from past decisions
may lack the information needed to understand how machine-generated
insights shaped the outcome.

Over time, this opacity can erode institutional trust.

Accountability depends not only on the integrity of professionals but
also on the transparency of the systems within which they operate. When
decision processes become difficult to trace, observers may begin to
question whether decisions are being made responsibly, even when
professionals act in good faith. The inability to explain how
machine-generated insight influenced reasoning creates uncertainty about
how decisions truly emerge.

Addressing this challenge requires recognising that artificial
intelligence does not merely produce outputs; it participates in the
environment where reasoning unfolds. If that participation remains
invisible, governance mechanisms cannot fully account for its effects.

The task for institutions is therefore not simply to regulate the
deployment of AI systems, but to ensure that the **influence of
machine-generated insight within decision processes remains visible and
accountable**.

The next section examines how this challenge is beginning to create a
broader transparency problem within institutional decision systems.

### **The emerging transparency problem in institutional decision systems**

Transparency has long been regarded as one of the central foundations of
responsible decision-making within institutions. Whether in scientific
research, public administration, healthcare, or corporate governance,
the legitimacy of decisions often depends on the ability of others to
understand how those decisions were reached. Transparency allows
reasoning to be examined, questioned, and improved. It enables
institutions to demonstrate that decisions were made thoughtfully,
responsibly, and in accordance with established standards.

Historically, transparency has been supported by the structure of
professional decision systems themselves. When decisions were produced
primarily through human reasoning, institutions developed documentation
practices that captured the most important elements of that reasoning. A
research paper explains the methods used to analyse data and the logic
that connects evidence to conclusions. A clinical record documents the
observations, tests, and interpretations that led to a diagnosis. A
policy report describes the arguments and evidence that informed a
particular recommendation.

These forms of documentation do not capture every moment of thinking
that occurred during the decision process, but they usually provide
enough information for others to understand how the final conclusion was
reached.

The introduction of artificial intelligence into professional reasoning
environments complicates this structure.

When machine-generated insights influence how professionals explore a
problem, the reasoning process begins to include interactions that may
not appear in traditional forms of documentation. A professional may
consult an intelligent system during the early stages of analysis, using
its outputs to explore possibilities, organise information, or generate
alternative interpretations. These interactions can shape how the
problem is framed and which lines of inquiry receive attention.

Yet such interactions often leave no trace in the formal record of the
decision.

The final documentation may describe the reasoning that ultimately
supports the decision, but it may not reveal the earlier interactions
that influenced the direction of that reasoning. Observers reviewing the
record see a coherent narrative of human judgement supported by
evidence, while the exploratory dialogue between human and machine that
helped shape that narrative remains invisible.

This phenomenon introduces what can be described as a **transparency
gap** within institutional decision systems.

The gap arises because existing documentation practices were designed
for environments in which the reasoning process was primarily human.
When professionals gathered information and analysed it independently,
the key elements of their reasoning could be captured through reports,
notes, and explanations. Artificial intelligence alters this environment
by introducing an additional source of analytical influence that may
operate informally within the reasoning process.

In many cases, professionals do not intentionally hide these
interactions. They may simply regard them as part of their exploratory
thinking, similar to reading background material or discussing ideas
with colleagues. Because these interactions occur during the early
stages of reasoning, before a formal conclusion has been reached, they
may not seem relevant to the final documentation.

From an institutional perspective, however, these interactions matter.

When intelligent systems influence which possibilities are considered,
how information is summarised, or which patterns attract attention, they
become part of the broader system that produced the decision. Even if
the professional ultimately exercises independent judgement, the
machine's contribution may have shaped the trajectory of the reasoning
process in ways that are difficult to reconstruct later.

As artificial intelligence becomes more deeply embedded in professional
environments, this transparency gap is likely to widen.

Professionals are increasingly able to consult intelligent systems at
any stage of their work. A researcher may generate summaries while
reviewing literature, explore patterns in data through machine-generated
suggestions, and refine interpretations through additional queries. A
policymaker may consult an AI system repeatedly while drafting analysis,
exploring different perspectives and testing alternative formulations of
an argument. Each interaction may influence how the problem is
understood, yet only the final reasoning may appear in the official
record.

The more frequently such interactions occur, the harder it becomes to
determine how machine-generated insight influenced the development of a
decision.

This challenge has important implications for institutional
accountability. If the influence of AI remains invisible, organisations
may struggle to evaluate the integrity of their decision processes. Peer
reviewers may not know which analytical suggestions originated from
human reasoning and which emerged from machine-generated analysis.
Auditors examining a decision may find it difficult to determine whether
relevant factors were considered independently or suggested by an
algorithmic system.

In environments where decisions carry significant consequences, such
uncertainty can weaken confidence in institutional processes.

Transparency does not require that every interaction between humans and
machines be recorded in exhaustive detail. Professionals need space for
exploratory thinking, and reasoning often develops through informal
investigation. However, institutions must still ensure that the key
influences shaping a decision remain visible enough to allow meaningful
review.

Achieving this balance requires recognising that transparency in the age
of artificial intelligence cannot rely solely on traditional
documentation practices. When machine-generated insights become part of
the reasoning process, institutions must consider how their decision
systems should evolve so that these interactions remain visible in
appropriate ways.

This does not necessarily mean recording every query made to an
intelligent system or preserving every intermediate suggestion generated
during analysis. Instead, it requires identifying the points in the
decision process where machine-generated insight meaningfully shapes
reasoning and ensuring that those influences are captured in a way that
allows others to understand how the decision environment operated.

In effect, institutions must begin to treat interactions between humans
and intelligent systems as part of the **decision infrastructure**
rather than as informal background activity.

Recognising this shift is a critical step toward addressing the
transparency challenges introduced by artificial intelligence. When the
presence of machine-generated insight becomes visible within decision
systems, institutions can begin to design governance mechanisms that
ensure accountability and trust remain intact.

The broader implication is that transparency in hybrid intelligence
environments must extend beyond the final explanation of a decision. It
must also consider the **systems of interaction through which reasoning
unfolds**.

Understanding how those systems operate is essential for ensuring that
decisions produced within hybrid intelligence environments remain
accountable, reviewable, and worthy of institutional trust.

[]{#_67mxxdranbsy .anchor}**Chapter 4 --- When Human--AI Systems Fail**

###  ******Why intelligent systems fail even when the technology works**

When discussions about artificial intelligence focus on failure, the
immediate assumption is often that something must have gone wrong with
the technology itself. A model may produce an incorrect output, a
dataset may contain bias, or a system may misinterpret information in a
way that leads to an undesirable result. In these cases, the failure
appears to be technical. The system did not perform as expected, and the
solution seems straightforward: improve the model, refine the training
data, or adjust the algorithm.

Yet many of the most significant problems associated with artificial
intelligence arise even when the technology performs exactly as
designed.

A system may generate accurate summaries, identify patterns correctly,
or produce plausible suggestions, and still contribute to decisions that
later appear flawed. In such situations, the underlying technology may
not have malfunctioned at all. The issue lies not with the system's
capability, but with how that capability interacts with human judgement
and organisational processes.

To understand why this happens, it is helpful to reconsider what we mean
when we refer to an "intelligent system."

Artificial intelligence does not operate in isolation. It functions
within a broader environment composed of human decision-makers,
institutional procedures, available information, and the workflows
through which reasoning unfolds. The outputs generated by the system are
interpreted, evaluated, and acted upon by people who bring their own
expertise, assumptions, and contextual understanding to the situation.

In other words, what we often describe as an AI system is actually part
of a larger **human--AI decision system**.

Within this larger system, the machine contributes analytical
suggestions, but the meaning and consequences of those suggestions are
determined by the human and institutional context in which they are
used. If the surrounding system is poorly designed, the interaction
between human judgement and machine-generated insight can produce
problematic outcomes even when the technology itself functions
perfectly.

Consider a simple example. An analytical model identifies a statistical
pattern within a large dataset and presents it as a potentially
significant relationship. The model has done exactly what it was
designed to do: detect patterns that might be difficult for humans to
see. A professional reviewing the output notices the pattern and decides
to investigate it further. Over time, the investigation develops into a
conclusion that appears convincing but later proves misleading because
the pattern reflected an underlying bias in the data.

In this situation, the technology did not fail in the conventional
sense. It detected a pattern accurately according to the data available
to it. The problem emerged because the reasoning system that interpreted
the output did not sufficiently question whether the pattern represented
a meaningful insight or an artefact of the dataset. The failure occurred
within the interaction between human judgement and machine-generated
analysis.

Similar dynamics appear across many professional environments.

A clinician may consider a diagnostic possibility highlighted by an
analytical system. The system has correctly identified a potential match
based on available data, but the clinician may give the suggestion more
weight than it deserves because it appears to be supported by a
sophisticated model. A researcher may explore a line of inquiry first
suggested by an AI-generated summary without recognising that other
possibilities were equally plausible but less prominently presented. A
policymaker may rely on machine-generated analysis that emphasises
certain patterns while overlooking contextual factors that the system
could not incorporate.

In each of these cases, the technology performs its intended function.
The difficulty arises from how the human--AI system interprets and
integrates that function into the broader decision process.

These situations reveal an important characteristic of intelligent
systems: their effectiveness depends not only on their technical
accuracy but also on the **structure of the decision environment in
which they operate**.

Even highly capable technologies can contribute to flawed outcomes if
the surrounding system does not provide mechanisms for careful
interpretation, critical evaluation, and contextual judgement.
Conversely, less sophisticated technologies can support responsible
decision-making when they are embedded within workflows that encourage
reflection and scrutiny.

This observation shifts the focus of analysis away from technology
alone.

When intelligent systems produce problematic outcomes, it is tempting to
search for flaws in the algorithm or the dataset. Sometimes such flaws
do exist, and improving the technology may indeed reduce certain risks.
But focusing exclusively on the technical components of artificial
intelligence overlooks the fact that most decisions influenced by AI
emerge from **complex interactions between humans and machines**.

If those interactions are poorly structured, errors may occur even when
the underlying technology behaves exactly as expected.

For this reason, evaluating the reliability of intelligent systems
requires examining the broader system in which they operate. How are
machine-generated suggestions introduced into the decision process? How
are they interpreted by professionals? What mechanisms exist for
questioning their reliability or comparing them with alternative
interpretations? And how are these interactions documented so that the
reasoning process can later be understood?

These questions concern not the internal workings of an algorithm but
the **design of the decision environment**.

Human--AI decision systems fail most often not because machines produce
incorrect outputs, but because the systems surrounding those machines
fail to guide how their outputs should be interpreted. When
machine-generated insights are accepted without sufficient scrutiny,
when contextual factors are overlooked, or when the reasoning process
remains undocumented, the resulting decision may appear flawed even
though the technology itself behaved as intended.

Recognising this distinction is essential for understanding the
governance challenges introduced by artificial intelligence.

If failures are interpreted solely as technical problems, organisations
will focus their efforts on improving models, adjusting datasets, or
refining algorithms. While these improvements may be valuable, they
address only part of the challenge. The deeper issue lies in how
intelligent systems are embedded within the processes through which
humans make decisions.

In other words, the reliability of AI-assisted decisions depends as much
on **system design** as on technological capability.

The next section explores this distinction more directly by examining
the difference between **technical failure** and **system failure** in
human--AI decision environments.

### **The difference between technical failure and system failure**

When a technological system produces an undesirable outcome, the
instinctive response is often to look for a technical explanation.
Perhaps the algorithm was flawed, the model was poorly trained, or the
data used by the system contained errors. In many fields, technological
failures can indeed be traced back to such causes. A malfunctioning
sensor, a coding error, or an inaccurate dataset can all lead to
incorrect outputs.

Artificial intelligence systems are no exception. Models may produce
misleading results because the data they were trained on is incomplete
or biased. Algorithms may perform poorly in situations that differ from
the conditions under which they were originally developed. Technical
limitations can therefore play a genuine role in problematic outcomes.

Yet focusing exclusively on these kinds of issues can obscure a deeper
and often more important distinction: the difference between **technical
failure** and **system failure**.

A **technical failure** occurs when a technological component does not
perform according to its design. A software bug causes incorrect
calculations. A dataset introduces bias that distorts predictions. A
system produces outputs that are demonstrably inaccurate according to
its intended function. In these situations, the failure originates
within the technology itself.

Technical failures are typically addressed through technical solutions.
Engineers refine algorithms, improve datasets, adjust parameters, or
redesign system components so that the technology performs more
reliably. Over time, such improvements can significantly reduce certain
kinds of error.

However, many problematic outcomes associated with artificial
intelligence do not originate from technical malfunction. Instead, they
arise from the **way technology interacts with the human and
organisational systems surrounding it**. In these cases, the technology
performs exactly as designed, but the overall system within which it
operates fails to produce a responsible outcome.

This is what can be described as **system failure**.

System failure occurs when the interaction between humans, technology,
and institutional processes produces a problematic result even though
the individual components may be functioning correctly. The technology
generates outputs according to its design, the professional reviewing
those outputs acts in good faith, and the organisation follows its
established procedures. Yet the outcome still proves flawed because the
system as a whole was not structured to manage the interaction
effectively.

To see the difference more clearly, consider a simple analogy from
aviation. If an aircraft's navigation instrument provides incorrect
readings due to a malfunction, the issue represents a technical failure.
Engineers must repair or replace the instrument so that it functions
properly.

But imagine a different situation. The instrument works perfectly, yet
the cockpit procedures do not require pilots to cross-check its readings
against other sources of information. If the pilot relies too heavily on
the instrument without verifying its output, the aircraft may still be
placed in danger---even though the instrument itself was functioning as
intended. In this case, the failure lies not in the technology but in
the **system of interaction between technology and human judgement**.

Human--AI decision systems exhibit similar dynamics.

An analytical model may generate a pattern that appears statistically
significant. The model has performed correctly according to its design.
A professional reviewing the output may interpret the pattern as
meaningful and incorporate it into their reasoning. The decision that
follows may later prove problematic because the pattern was influenced
by factors that neither the model nor the professional initially
recognised.

In such a scenario, it may be tempting to attribute the problem to the
model. Yet the deeper issue may lie elsewhere. The system surrounding
the model may not have provided sufficient mechanisms for questioning
the output, comparing it with alternative explanations, or examining the
broader context in which the pattern appeared. The technology did what
it was designed to do, but the system did not adequately support
responsible interpretation.

This distinction becomes particularly important as artificial
intelligence becomes more integrated into professional environments.

When institutions encounter problematic outcomes involving AI, they may
focus primarily on improving the technical components of the system.
Models are retrained, datasets are expanded, and algorithms are refined.
These steps can certainly improve performance, but they do not
necessarily address the structural conditions that allowed the
problematic outcome to occur in the first place.

If the interaction between human judgement and machine-generated insight
remains poorly structured, similar problems may arise again even after
technical improvements have been made.

Recognising the difference between technical failure and system failure
therefore changes how organisations approach the governance of
artificial intelligence. Instead of asking only whether the technology
works correctly, institutions must also ask whether the **decision
environment surrounding the technology is designed appropriately**.

This broader perspective leads to a different set of questions. How are
machine-generated insights introduced into professional reasoning
processes? What expectations guide how those insights should be
evaluated? How do professionals distinguish between plausible
suggestions and misleading patterns? And how does the organisation
ensure that the reasoning process remains transparent and accountable?

These questions cannot be answered solely through improvements in
algorithms or data. They require attention to the design of the systems
within which human--AI collaboration occurs.

In many cases, failures attributed to artificial intelligence are in
fact failures of system design. The technology functions correctly, but
the surrounding structures do not adequately guide how its outputs
should be interpreted, challenged, or integrated into decision-making.

Understanding this distinction is essential for the development of
effective governance approaches. If institutions treat every problematic
outcome as a technical failure, they may overlook the deeper structural
issues that shape how human and machine intelligence interact.
Conversely, recognising system failure allows organisations to focus on
designing decision environments that support responsible collaboration
between humans and AI.

This shift in perspective sets the stage for a more systematic analysis
of how human--AI decision systems can break down. In the next section,
we introduce a framework for understanding the **recurring patterns
through which such failures occur**.

### **Preview of the failure modes of human--AI decision systems**

Recognising the difference between technical failure and system failure
leads to an important realisation: many of the risks associated with
artificial intelligence do not arise from faulty algorithms but from the
way human--AI decision systems are structured. The interaction between
human judgement and machine-generated insight can create patterns of
behaviour that gradually undermine responsible decision-making, even
when both the technology and the professionals using it are acting in
good faith.

These patterns do not appear randomly. Across different professional
environments, similar dynamics tend to recur whenever intelligent
systems become embedded in decision processes. Over time, certain types
of breakdown begin to appear with surprising consistency. Professionals
may place too much trust in machine-generated suggestions, overlook
contextual information that lies outside the model's perspective, or
lose visibility into how decisions are being shaped by algorithmic
outputs.

These recurring dynamics can be understood as **failure modes** within
human--AI decision systems.

A failure mode is not a single mistake or isolated error. Instead, it is
a characteristic pattern through which a system tends to break down
under certain conditions. In engineering and safety analysis,
identifying failure modes is a way of understanding how complex systems
behave when different components interact in unintended ways. By
recognising these patterns in advance, organisations can design
processes that reduce the likelihood of failure and improve the
resilience of the system as a whole.

Human--AI decision environments exhibit their own set of such patterns.

One of the most common arises when professionals gradually begin to
defer to machine-generated outputs without fully examining their
limitations. Intelligent systems often produce results that appear
precise, structured, and confident. Over time, this presentation can
create the impression that the system possesses a form of authority.
Even experienced professionals may begin to treat machine-generated
suggestions as especially reliable simply because they emerge from a
sophisticated technological system. This subtle shift in perception can
lead to what might be called **automation drift**, where human judgement
gradually yields more influence to machine-generated analysis.

Another pattern appears when the role of artificial intelligence within
a decision process becomes difficult to observe. As intelligent systems
are consulted during the exploratory stages of reasoning, their
contributions may remain undocumented. A machine-generated suggestion
may guide the direction of investigation, yet the final decision record
may not reveal that the suggestion originated from a system rather than
from human reasoning. Over time, this invisibility can obscure how
decisions are actually being shaped.

A third pattern involves the interpretation of machine-generated
insights without sufficient contextual understanding. Artificial
intelligence systems operate by identifying patterns within data or
generating outputs based on statistical relationships learned during
training. These outputs may appear meaningful, yet they do not
necessarily reflect the full complexity of the real-world environment in
which the decision occurs. If professionals interpret these outputs
without considering contextual factors that lie beyond the system's
perspective, the reasoning process may gradually diverge from the
realities it is intended to address.

These examples illustrate a broader point: the reliability of human--AI
decision systems depends not only on the capabilities of the technology
but also on the **structures through which its outputs are interpreted
and integrated into professional reasoning**.

Without clear structures, several risks emerge simultaneously.
Machine-generated insights may be accepted without adequate scrutiny.
Important contextual knowledge may be overlooked. Responsibility for
decisions may become blurred when multiple sources of analysis
contribute to the reasoning process. And the documentation that supports
institutional accountability may fail to capture the true dynamics of
how conclusions were reached.

What makes these patterns particularly important is that they often
develop gradually. They do not usually appear as dramatic failures in
which a system suddenly collapses. Instead, they emerge through subtle
shifts in behaviour and perception. Professionals may begin by
consulting intelligent systems cautiously, using them as supplementary
tools. Over time, as familiarity with the technology grows, its outputs
may begin to shape reasoning more directly. The change can occur without
any explicit decision to alter the structure of the decision process.

Because these dynamics unfold gradually, they may remain unnoticed until
a significant problem arises.

When such a problem does occur---perhaps a flawed research conclusion, a
problematic policy recommendation, or a decision that appears difficult
to justify---organisations may initially search for a technical
explanation. Attention may focus on the algorithm, the dataset, or the
software platform involved. Yet the deeper cause often lies in the
interaction between human judgement and machine-generated insight rather
than in the technology alone.

Understanding these recurring patterns is therefore essential for the
governance of artificial intelligence. Institutions must move beyond
viewing intelligent systems as isolated technologies and begin examining
them as components of broader decision environments. Only by analysing
how these environments behave under real conditions can organisations
identify where vulnerabilities emerge.

In the chapters that follow, we will examine these vulnerabilities more
systematically. Several recurring failure modes can be observed across
human--AI decision systems. These include patterns such as the gradual
transfer of authority from humans to machines, the invisibility of
algorithmic influence within reasoning processes, the erosion of
professional judgement through over-reliance on automated analysis, and
the diffusion of responsibility within complex decision environments.

These patterns do not imply that artificial intelligence is inherently
unsafe or incompatible with responsible decision-making. On the
contrary, intelligent systems can significantly expand the analytical
capabilities of individuals and institutions when used thoughtfully. The
purpose of identifying failure modes is not to discourage the use of AI
but to understand how human--AI systems behave so that they can be
designed more carefully.

Recognising these patterns marks an important step toward a more
systematic approach to governing artificial intelligence. If
institutions can identify how human--AI decision systems tend to fail,
they can begin to design structures that make those failures less likely
and easier to detect.

This insight leads directly to the discipline introduced in this book.
Human--AI Governance Engineering seeks to understand how decision
systems involving both human judgement and machine intelligence can be
designed so that their strengths reinforce one another while their
vulnerabilities are carefully managed.

[]{#_ljrs6eb8tx1 .anchor}**\**

# **Chapter 5 --- The Governance Gap** 

###  ******Why traditional governance systems were built for human decision-making**

Governance systems exist to ensure that decisions are made responsibly
within institutions. They define how authority is exercised, how
accountability is maintained, and how reasoning can be reviewed when
important choices affect individuals, organisations, or society more
broadly. In professions such as medicine, law, research, engineering,
and public administration, governance structures have evolved over many
decades to guide how decisions should be made and how those decisions
should be justified.

These structures were built around a fundamental assumption: **that the
core act of reasoning belongs to a human professional**.

Within this assumption, the decision process is understood as something
that occurs primarily within the mind of the decision-maker. A
professional gathers information, interprets evidence, applies
experience and expertise, and eventually reaches a conclusion.
Governance systems are designed to ensure that this reasoning is
exercised responsibly and that it can be examined by others when
necessary.

As a result, traditional governance structures focus on several key
elements that support responsible human judgement.

First, they establish **clear responsibility**. When a decision is made,
there must be a person or identifiable group accountable for that
decision. In medicine, a clinician is responsible for a diagnosis or
treatment plan. In research, a principal investigator is accountable for
the integrity of the study. In public administration, an official or
committee is responsible for the policies that are enacted.
Responsibility is attached to individuals because it is assumed that
those individuals exercised the judgement that produced the decision.

Second, governance systems require **documentation of reasoning**.
Important decisions are rarely accepted without explanation. A
scientific paper describes the methods and evidence supporting a
conclusion. A clinical record explains the observations and reasoning
that led to a diagnosis. A policy report outlines the arguments and
analysis that informed a recommendation. These records allow others to
understand how the decision was reached and provide a basis for review
or critique.

Third, institutions develop **review mechanisms** that allow decisions
to be evaluated. Peer review in research, case review in medicine, and
oversight committees in governance structures all exist to examine the
reasoning behind decisions and ensure that professional standards have
been maintained. The ability to reconstruct the reasoning process is
essential for these mechanisms to function.

Finally, governance systems support **professional capability**.
Training, certification, and continuing professional development ensure
that individuals entrusted with decision-making possess the knowledge
and judgement required to fulfil their responsibilities. Governance
therefore assumes that responsible decisions depend on the competence
and integrity of the professionals making them.

Taken together, these elements form a coherent structure. Responsibility
is assigned to identifiable individuals. Those individuals explain the
reasoning behind their decisions. Institutions provide mechanisms to
review and evaluate that reasoning. And professional training ensures
that those exercising judgement are capable of doing so responsibly.

This structure has proven remarkably resilient across many domains of
professional life.

Even as technologies have evolved, the fundamental architecture of
governance has remained largely consistent. New tools have been
introduced to support professional work---databases, statistical
software, modelling platforms, and digital communication systems---but
these tools have generally been treated as instruments used by human
decision-makers rather than as participants in the reasoning process
itself.

Under this model, governance does not need to account for how tools
think. It only needs to account for how people use them.

If a spreadsheet produces a calculation, the professional using it
remains responsible for interpreting the result. If a database retrieves
information, the professional decides how that information should
influence their reasoning. The tools assist the process, but they do not
introduce new sources of interpretation that must be governed in their
own right.

Artificial intelligence challenges this assumption.

Unlike traditional information tools, intelligent systems can generate
analyses, interpretations, and suggestions that resemble elements of
reasoning. They do not merely retrieve information; they propose ways of
understanding that information. When professionals consult such systems
during their work, the machine's output becomes part of the environment
in which reasoning unfolds.

Yet traditional governance structures were not designed for such
environments.

They assume that the key elements of reasoning originate from the human
decision-maker and can therefore be documented and reviewed as part of
the professional's explanation of their judgement. When
machine-generated insight becomes involved in shaping that judgement,
the reasoning process becomes more complex. Some influences may
originate outside the human decision-maker, and the interaction between
human interpretation and machine-generated suggestion may not always be
visible in traditional forms of documentation.

This does not mean that traditional governance systems are obsolete. The
principles they embody---accountability, transparency, review, and
professional competence---remain essential. However, these systems were
designed for a world in which decision-making was fundamentally human.

As artificial intelligence becomes more integrated into professional
environments, the structure of reasoning itself begins to change.
Decisions increasingly emerge from interactions between human judgement
and machine-generated insight. Governance systems built around purely
human reasoning must therefore adapt to account for this new form of
collaboration.

Understanding why existing governance models were built around human
decision-making is the first step toward recognising the **governance
gap** that artificial intelligence introduces.

###  ******Why AI disrupts existing governance models**

Traditional governance models function effectively when the structure of
decision-making is relatively stable. Responsibility is assigned to
identifiable individuals, reasoning is documented through reports or
records, and institutional oversight examines whether the professional
judgement exercised in a decision meets established standards. This
model works because the central assumption behind it is clear: the
reasoning process that produced the decision originates from human
judgement.

Artificial intelligence challenges that assumption.

When intelligent systems begin to generate interpretations, suggestions,
and analytical insights, the reasoning process no longer originates
exclusively from human cognition. Instead, it unfolds through
interactions between human judgement and machine-generated analysis.
Even when the final decision remains human, the path that leads to that
decision may include influences produced by systems that operate outside
the traditional governance structure.

This shift disrupts existing governance models in several ways.

The first disruption concerns **the origin of reasoning**. In
traditional professional practice, the reasoning that leads to a
decision is expected to be explainable by the professional responsible
for it. A researcher explains the interpretation of their data, a
clinician explains the logic behind a diagnosis, and a policymaker
explains the analysis supporting a recommendation. These explanations
assume that the professional can reconstruct the reasoning process that
produced the decision.

When artificial intelligence contributes to that reasoning process,
reconstruction becomes more complex. A professional may consult an
intelligent system multiple times while exploring a problem, using its
outputs to generate possibilities, compare perspectives, or summarise
information. These interactions can shape the trajectory of reasoning,
yet the professional may not fully recall every suggestion generated
during the process or how each influenced the development of their
thinking.

As a result, the reasoning process may become partially opaque even to
the person responsible for the decision.

The second disruption concerns **the structure of accountability**.
Governance systems typically assign responsibility to individuals or
committees because it is assumed that these actors exercise the
judgement that determines the outcome. Artificial intelligence
complicates this structure by introducing machine-generated insight into
the reasoning environment.

If a professional consults an intelligent system that suggests a
particular interpretation of evidence, and that interpretation later
proves problematic, responsibility still rests with the professional who
accepted the suggestion. Yet the presence of machine-generated analysis
raises questions about how that suggestion influenced the reasoning
process. The decision remains human in its authority, but the reasoning
environment that shaped it now includes elements that governance systems
were not originally designed to track.

The third disruption involves **documentation and transparency**.
Traditional governance relies on the ability to review the reasoning
behind decisions. Reports, records, and formal explanations allow others
to understand how conclusions were reached. Artificial intelligence
introduces interactions that may not appear in these records. A
machine-generated suggestion may guide the direction of analysis, yet
the final documentation may present the reasoning as though it emerged
entirely from human deliberation.

Over time, this can create a gap between the visible reasoning recorded
in institutional documentation and the actual process through which
decisions were formed.

The fourth disruption concerns **the pace and scale of analysis**.
Intelligent systems allow professionals to explore possibilities and
generate analytical outputs far more quickly than traditional methods
allowed. While this capability can greatly enhance productivity and
insight, it also means that the reasoning process may involve a large
number of machine-generated interactions that occur rapidly during the
exploration of a problem.

Governance systems designed for slower, more deliberate processes of
human reasoning may struggle to capture or evaluate these interactions.
Oversight mechanisms that rely on reviewing final documentation may not
provide visibility into how machine-generated insights influenced
earlier stages of analysis.

A fifth disruption arises from **the evolving nature of professional
expertise**. In traditional governance models, professional authority
rests on the assumption that trained experts apply their knowledge
directly to the information available to them. Artificial intelligence
changes the way expertise is exercised. Professionals increasingly
operate as interpreters of machine-generated insight rather than as the
sole originators of analysis.

This shift does not eliminate the need for expertise, but it changes how
expertise is applied. Professionals must evaluate outputs produced by
systems whose internal processes may not be fully transparent.
Governance models that focus primarily on individual judgement may
therefore overlook the complex interaction between expertise and
machine-generated analysis that now shapes many decisions.

Taken together, these disruptions reveal a common pattern: artificial
intelligence does not simply introduce new tools into existing decision
environments. It **reshapes the architecture of those environments**.

Existing governance models assume that reasoning originates from
identifiable individuals, unfolds through human deliberation, and can be
documented in a way that allows others to reconstruct the decision
process. When machine-generated insights participate in reasoning, these
assumptions become less reliable. The reasoning process becomes
distributed across interactions between humans and machines, some of
which may occur informally during exploratory analysis.

The result is a growing mismatch between the structure of governance
systems and the structure of the decision environments they are meant to
govern.

This mismatch does not mean that traditional governance
principles---such as accountability, transparency, and professional
responsibility---are no longer valid. On the contrary, these principles
become even more important when intelligent systems influence
decision-making. What changes is the way those principles must be
implemented.

Institutions must begin to consider not only who is responsible for
decisions but also how the systems within which those decisions are
produced should be structured. They must examine how machine-generated
insights enter the reasoning process, how those insights are evaluated,
and how the interaction between human judgement and machine intelligence
can remain visible and accountable.

In other words, governance must move beyond regulating technologies
alone and begin addressing the **design of human--AI decision systems**.

Recognising this shift reveals the gap that artificial intelligence has
created within existing governance models. The principles of governance
remain essential, but the systems through which those principles are
applied must evolve.

The next section introduces a discipline intended to address this
challenge: **Human--AI Governance Engineering**.

###  ******Introducing the discipline of Human--AI Governance Engineering**

The emergence of artificial intelligence within professional decision
environments has revealed a structural gap in existing governance
approaches. Institutions possess ethical frameworks, regulatory
policies, and professional standards intended to guide responsible
behaviour. These instruments articulate important principles, yet they
were developed for a world in which the core act of reasoning belonged
entirely to human professionals.

As intelligent systems begin to contribute analytical insight within
decision processes, governance can no longer focus solely on the
behaviour of individuals or the regulation of technologies in isolation.
Instead, it must address the **systems through which human judgement and
machine-generated insight interact**.

This shift in perspective introduces the need for a new kind of
discipline.

That discipline can be described as **Human--AI Governance
Engineering**.

Human--AI Governance Engineering is concerned with the design of
decision systems in which human and machine intelligence operate
together. It focuses not only on the ethical use of artificial
intelligence or the regulation of technological systems, but on the
structure of the environments in which reasoning occurs when intelligent
machines participate in analysis.

The central premise of the discipline is simple but far-reaching: when
machines contribute to human reasoning, governance must be embedded
within the **architecture of the decision system itself**.

This approach differs in important ways from existing discussions about
AI governance.

Many governance efforts concentrate on the behaviour of artificial
intelligence technologies. They ask whether algorithms are fair, whether
data is used responsibly, or whether systems operate within regulatory
boundaries. These questions are essential, but they address only part of
the challenge. They focus primarily on the properties of the technology
rather than on the broader system within which the technology operates.

Human--AI Governance Engineering begins from a different starting point.
It asks how decision environments should be designed so that the
interaction between human judgement and machine-generated insight
remains transparent, accountable, and aligned with professional
responsibility.

This perspective treats artificial intelligence not merely as a tool to
be regulated but as a component within a larger system of reasoning.

Within such systems, several elements interact simultaneously. Human
professionals bring domain knowledge, contextual awareness, and ethical
judgement. Intelligent systems contribute analytical capabilities that
allow patterns, summaries, or possibilities to be generated quickly from
large bodies of information. Institutional structures establish the
procedures through which decisions are reviewed and validated.

When these elements interact effectively, they can produce decision
environments that combine the strengths of both human and machine
intelligence. When they interact poorly, the resulting system may
obscure responsibility, weaken transparency, or encourage overreliance
on machine-generated outputs.

Human--AI Governance Engineering therefore focuses on **how these
interactions are structured**.

Rather than asking only how AI systems should be built, the discipline
asks how decision workflows should be designed. It examines how
machine-generated insights enter the reasoning process, how
professionals interpret and evaluate those insights, and how
institutions can ensure that the reasoning process remains visible and
accountable.

In practical terms, this means shifting governance thinking from rules
and policies toward **system architecture**.

Policies define expectations for responsible behaviour. Governance
engineering defines the structures that make responsible behaviour
possible in practice. It designs workflows that preserve human
accountability even when intelligent systems contribute analysis. It
creates mechanisms through which interactions between humans and
machines can be documented and reviewed. And it ensures that
professional capability remains central to the interpretation of
machine-generated insight.

Seen from this perspective, artificial intelligence introduces not only
a technological challenge but also an institutional design challenge.

Human societies have long experience designing governance systems for
complex activities. In fields such as aviation, medicine, and
engineering, safety and accountability are achieved not only through
rules but through carefully structured processes. Checklists guide
pilots through critical procedures. Clinical protocols ensure that
medical decisions follow well-established diagnostic pathways.
Engineering standards require documentation and verification at each
stage of a project.

These systems succeed because governance is embedded within the
**workflow of practice**.

Human--AI Governance Engineering applies a similar principle to decision
environments involving artificial intelligence. Instead of relying
solely on policy statements that describe responsible behaviour, the
discipline seeks to design systems that make responsible human--AI
collaboration the natural outcome of how decisions are structured.

This does not mean replacing human judgement with rigid procedural
control. On the contrary, the discipline recognises that professional
reasoning must remain flexible, adaptive, and capable of responding to
complex situations. Governance engineering aims to support that
judgement by ensuring that the reasoning environment provides the
transparency, documentation, and oversight necessary for accountability.

The emergence of this discipline reflects a broader transformation in
the nature of decision systems.

As artificial intelligence becomes increasingly integrated into
professional life, institutions will need ways to ensure that the
collaboration between human judgement and machine intelligence
strengthens rather than weakens responsible decision-making. Achieving
this goal requires more than technical expertise or ethical aspiration.
It requires the deliberate design of systems that integrate governance
into the structure of reasoning itself.

Human--AI Governance Engineering provides a framework for undertaking
that work.

The chapters that follow explore the principles, concepts, and system
architectures that underpin this emerging discipline. They examine how
hybrid intelligence environments function, how failures arise within
human--AI decision systems, and how institutions can design governance
structures capable of supporting accountable collaboration between human
judgement and machine intelligence.

The challenge introduced by artificial intelligence is not simply to
build more powerful machines. It is to ensure that the systems within
which those machines operate remain worthy of the trust placed in
professional and institutional decision-making.
